=== mlsys1-01 | The accelerator as a cost model: FLOPs, bandwidth and the roofline
--- teach
In AI from scratch you wrote matrix multiply as loops over lists, and a whole transformer block on top of it. In Parallel Computing you predicted a program's running time from its work and span before running it. This course puts the two together. Real models are trained on thousands of accelerators, and every decision about that training (batch size, number format, how to split the model) starts with one question: **how long should this piece of work take on this chip?** This lesson builds the cost model that answers it. Every later lab reuses it.

### Counting the work: FLOPs

A **[[FLOP|flop]]** is one floating-point operation: one addition or one multiplication. Training is almost all matrix multiplies, so start there.

Multiply an m×k matrix by a k×n matrix. The result has m·n entries. Each entry is a dot product of length k: k multiplications and k additions. So the multiply costs **2·m·k·n FLOPs**.

A 4×3 matrix times a 3×5 matrix: 15 outputs, each a dot product of length 3, so 2 · 4 · 3 · 5 = 120 FLOPs. (Strictly it is k − 1 additions per entry, but every real system counts a multiply-add as 2 FLOPs, and so will we.)

### Counting the traffic: bytes

Compute units cannot work on numbers that sit in memory. Every operand has to travel from the accelerator's main memory to the compute units, and every result has to travel back. The accelerator's main memory is **[[HBM|hbm]]**, high-bandwidth memory: tens of gigabytes stacked right beside the chip.

For the same multiply, the least traffic possible is to read each input once and write the output once: m·k + k·n + m·n numbers. Each number takes some bytes: 4 in 32-bit floats, 2 in the 16-bit formats training uses. For the 4×3 by 3×5 multiply in 16-bit numbers that is 2 · (12 + 15 + 20) = 94 bytes.

### Two speeds, one time

An accelerator has two speeds that matter:

- its **peak FLOP/s**: how many FLOPs per second its compute units can do at most;
- its **memory bandwidth**: how many bytes per second can move between HBM and the compute units.

Think of a kitchen. The chef chops at some speed, and the pantry delivers ingredients at some speed. A dish that needs a lot of chopping per ingredient is limited by the chef. A dish that is mostly fetching is limited by the pantry. Good kitchens fetch the next ingredients while the chef works, so the slower of the two sets the pace.

That gives the model this course uses. A kernel (one piece of work launched on the accelerator) that does F FLOPs and moves B bytes takes at least

**time = max(F / peak, B / bandwidth)**

It is a **lower bound**: it assumes computing and moving overlap perfectly and both run at full speed. Real kernels are slower. The model still tells you which wall you are against, and how far away the other one is.

Here are the assumptions for the course's accelerator, called A-1. They are close to a 2023-era flagship data-centre training chip:

| | A-1 |
|---|---|
| peak (dense 16-bit) | 1.0 × 10¹⁵ FLOP/s, a **petaFLOP/s** |
| HBM bandwidth | 3.0 × 10¹² bytes/s, 3 TB/s |
| HBM capacity | 80 × 10⁹ bytes, 80 GB |

### Arithmetic intensity

Divide the two counts. The **arithmetic intensity** of a kernel is

**I = F / B**, in FLOPs per byte:

how much work it does with each byte it moves. It is a property of the algorithm and the shapes, not of the chip.

An elementwise kernel, such as GELU over a 2048 × 16384 tensor of 16-bit numbers, reads each number once and writes it once (4 bytes) and does about 10 FLOPs on it. Its intensity is 10 / 4 = 2.5 FLOPs per byte.

A matrix multiply reuses every number many times. For m = 2048 tokens times a 4096 × 4096 weight in 16-bit numbers:

```python
peak = 1.0e15        # FLOP/s
bw = 3.0e12          # bytes/s
work = 2 * 2048 * 4096 * 4096
moved = 2 * (2048 * 4096 + 4096 * 4096 + 2048 * 4096)
print(work / moved)                   # 1024.0 FLOPs per byte
print(work / peak * 1e6)              # 68.72 microseconds of compute
print(moved / bw * 1e6)               # 22.37 microseconds of memory traffic
```

The compute takes longer, so this multiply is **compute-bound**: about 69 µs on A-1. The GELU is **memory-bound**: 134 MB of traffic takes 44.7 µs, while its 0.34 GFLOP would take 0.34 µs.

### The roofline

The fastest a kernel of intensity I can run is

**attainable FLOP/s = min(peak, I × bandwidth)**

Drawn with intensity along the bottom (on a log scale), it is a sloped line, I × bandwidth, that meets a flat line, the peak: a roof. This picture is the **[[roofline model|roofline]]**. The corner where they meet is the **[[ridge point|ridge]]**:

**ridge = peak / bandwidth**

For A-1 it is 10¹⁵ / (3 × 10¹²) ≈ 333 FLOPs per byte. A kernel to the left of the ridge is memory-bound: buying more FLOP/s does nothing for it. A kernel to the right is compute-bound: faster memory does nothing for it.

### Why shapes decide everything

For a multiply of m tokens by a d × d weight in 16-bit numbers, F = 2·m·d² and B = 2·(2·m·d + d²). When m is much smaller than d, B is about 2·d², so **I ≈ m**: the intensity is roughly the number of tokens multiplied at once. The weight is read once and used m times.

So 16 tokens times a 4096 × 4096 weight has intensity about 15.9, far left of the ridge: it runs at under 5% of peak. That is why training uses batches of thousands of tokens per multiply, and why generating text one token at a time (ML Systems II) is a memory problem, not a compute problem.

### Where it is used

- **Before writing a kernel**, engineers compute its intensity and its roofline time. If a measured kernel takes 3 times its roofline time, there is room to win; if it is at 90%, stop.
- **Choosing a batch size**: below the ridge, a bigger batch is nearly free, because the weights are read once either way.
- **Fusing kernels**: a chain of elementwise kernels is memory-bound, so doing all of them in one pass over memory saves time. Compilers for ML do this constantly.
- **Model FLOPs utilization**, the efficiency number every training run reports (lesson 07), is measured against this peak.

**Watch out:**

- **Units.** A TFLOP is 10¹² FLOPs and a GB here is 10⁹ bytes. Mixing a petaFLOP/s with gigabytes per second without converting puts you off by a factor of a thousand or a million.
- **Bytes per number.** Intensity in FLOPs per *element* is not FLOPs per *byte*. The same kernel in 32-bit numbers has half the intensity of its 16-bit version.
- **Peak is a ceiling.** The model gives a lower bound on time. A vendor's peak may also assume structured sparsity or a smaller number format than the one you train in; read which.

::: context flop FLOPs and FLOP/s
FLOPs (a count of operations) and FLOP/s (operations per second) are easy to confuse, and papers often write "flops" for both. A useful habit: write the per-second one with a slash. Training budgets are counted in total FLOPs: a large model's whole training run is on the order of 10²⁴ to 10²⁵ FLOPs. Chips are rated in FLOP/s. Dividing one by the other, and by how efficiently the chips are used, gives the training time, which is lesson 07's calculation.
:::

::: context hbm Memory stacked beside the chip
High-bandwidth memory is DRAM built as a stack of thin dies, connected through thousands of vertical wires to the same package as the accelerator. The very wide connection is what gives terabytes per second, far more than the memory sticks of an ordinary computer. It is also expensive and limited in size: 80 GB is a lot for a chip, but a large model's weights, gradients and optimizer state need far more, which is why lessons 03 and 05 exist. Smaller, faster on-chip memory (SRAM, a few tens of megabytes) sits inside the chip itself; tiling, in this lesson's practice, is about using it well.
:::

::: context roofline A picture from 2009
Samuel Williams, Andrew Waterman and David Patterson published the roofline model in 2009 as a visual way to see what limits a program on a multicore chip. Plot attainable performance against arithmetic intensity, both on log scales: memory bandwidth draws a slanted line, peak compute a flat one.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 340 190" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="40" y1="160" x2="320" y2="160" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <line x1="40" y1="160" x2="40" y2="15" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="182" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">arithmetic intensity (FLOP per byte, log)</text>
  <text x="16" y="90" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44" transform="rotate(-90 16 90)">FLOP/s (log)</text>
  <polyline fill="none" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2.5" points="40,150 200,40 320,40"/>
  <line x1="200" y1="40" x2="200" y2="160" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-dasharray="4 3"/>
  <text x="200" y="30" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">ridge, about 333</text>
  <text x="70" y="110" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1">memory-bound</text>
  <text x="230" y="60" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1">compute-bound</text>
  <circle cx="86" cy="118" r="4" fill="#b4232c"/>

```
:::

::: context ridge The ridge in other units
The ridge point is the intensity at which a kernel stops waiting for memory. It has been rising for years, because compute has grown much faster than memory bandwidth: each new chip generation multiplies peak FLOP/s by more than it multiplies bandwidth. A higher ridge means more kernels fall on the memory-bound side, so reuse (big batches, tiling, fusion) matters more on every new chip, not less.
:::
--- task
Build the course's cost model. The starter has the `Accelerator` class with its four fields (`name`, `peak_flops` in FLOP/s, `mem_bw` in bytes per second, `mem_bytes`) and the course's accelerator `ACCEL`. Write:

- `matmul_flops(m, k, n)`: the FLOPs of an m×k times k×n multiply, counting a multiply-add as 2.
- `matmul_bytes(m, k, n, bytes_per)`: the least traffic of that multiply in bytes: both inputs read once and the output written once, `bytes_per` bytes each.

and four methods of `Accelerator`:

- `ridge()`: peak FLOP/s divided by bandwidth.
- `attainable(intensity)`: the roofline, min(peak, intensity × bandwidth).
- `time(flops, nbytes)`: the lower-bound time in seconds, max(flops / peak, nbytes / bandwidth).
- `bound(flops, nbytes)`: `"compute"` if the compute time is at least the memory time, otherwise `"memory"` (so a kernel exactly at the ridge counts as `"compute"`).

For example, `matmul_flops(4, 3, 5)` is `120`, `matmul_bytes(4, 3, 5, 2)` is `94`, and `ACCEL.ridge()` is about 333.3.
--- starter
class Accelerator:
    """A cost model of one accelerator: how fast it computes and how fast it moves bytes."""

    def __init__(self, name, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.name = name
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops   # FLOP per second, dense, in the training format
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw           # bytes per second between memory and compute
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes     # bytes of on-device memory

    def ridge(self):
        return 0.0

    def attainable(self, intensity):
        return 0.0

    def time(self, flops, nbytes):
        return 0.0

    def bound(self, flops, nbytes):
        return "memory"


def matmul_flops(m, k, n):
    return 0


def matmul_bytes(m, k, n, bytes_per):
    return 0


# The course's accelerator (an assumption close to a 2023-era flagship training chip).
ACCEL = Accelerator("A-1", 1.0e15, 3.0e12, 80e9)
--- solution
class Accelerator:
    """A cost model of one accelerator: how fast it computes and how fast it moves bytes."""

    def __init__(self, name, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.name = name
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops   # FLOP per second, dense, in the training format
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw           # bytes per second between memory and compute
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes     # bytes of on-device memory

    def ridge(self):
        return self.peak_flops / self.mem_bw

    def attainable(self, intensity):
        return min(self.peak_flops, intensity * self.mem_bw)

    def time(self, flops, nbytes):
        return max(flops / self.peak_flops, nbytes / self.mem_bw)

    def bound(self, flops, nbytes):
        if flops / self.peak_flops >= nbytes / self.mem_bw:
            return "compute"
        return "memory"


def matmul_flops(m, k, n):
    return 2 * m * k * n


def matmul_bytes(m, k, n, bytes_per):
    return bytes_per * (m * k + k * n + m * n)


# The course's accelerator (an assumption close to a 2023-era flagship training chip).
ACCEL = Accelerator("A-1", 1.0e15, 3.0e12, 80e9)
--- hint
Each output of the multiply is a dot product of length k, and there are m·n outputs. The traffic counts the m·k, k·n and m·n numbers once each.
--- hint
The methods are one line each, written from the formulas in the lesson using `self.peak_flops` and `self.mem_bw`. `bound` compares `flops / self.peak_flops` with `nbytes / self.mem_bw`.
--- hint
Use `>=` in `bound`, so a tie goes to `"compute"`.
--- check case | FLOPs and bytes of the small example
(matmul_flops(4, 3, 5), matmul_bytes(4, 3, 5, 2))
=> (120, 94)
--- check case | 32-bit numbers double the bytes, not the FLOPs
(matmul_flops(4, 3, 5), matmul_bytes(4, 3, 5, 4))
=> (120, 188)
--- check case | A big square multiply
(matmul_flops(8192, 8192, 8192), matmul_bytes(8192, 8192, 8192, 2))
=> (1099511627776, 402653184)
--- check test | A-1's ridge is about 333 FLOPs per byte
abs(ACCEL.ridge() - 1e15 / 3e12) < 1e-6
--- check test | Left of the ridge, the slope: intensity times bandwidth
abs(ACCEL.attainable(10) - 3e13) < 1.0
--- check test | Right of the ridge, the flat roof
ACCEL.attainable(1000) == 1e15 and ACCEL.attainable(333.4) == 1e15
--- check test | time is the larger of the two times
abs(ACCEL.time(2e12, 1e9) - 2e-3) < 1e-12 and abs(ACCEL.time(1e9, 3e9) - 1e-3) < 1e-12
--- check case | The big multiply is compute-bound; 16 tokens is memory-bound
(ACCEL.bound(matmul_flops(8192, 8192, 8192), matmul_bytes(8192, 8192, 8192, 2)), ACCEL.bound(matmul_flops(16, 4096, 4096), matmul_bytes(16, 4096, 4096, 2)))
=> ("compute", "memory")
--- check case | Exactly at the ridge counts as compute
(Accelerator("toy", 100, 10, 1).bound(100, 10), Accelerator("toy", 100, 10, 1).bound(99, 10))
=> ("compute", "memory")
--- check test | The lesson's numbers: 2048 tokens times a 4096 x 4096 weight
abs(ACCEL.time(matmul_flops(2048, 4096, 4096), matmul_bytes(2048, 4096, 4096, 2)) - 6.8719476736e-05) < 1e-12
--- check test | Works for another accelerator too
abs(Accelerator("slow", 2e14, 1e12, 16e9).ridge() - 200) < 1e-9 and abs(Accelerator("slow", 2e14, 1e12, 16e9).time(4e14, 1e12) - 2.0) < 1e-12

+++ question | What the ridge tells you
--- ask
A kernel has arithmetic intensity 40 FLOPs per byte, and A-1's ridge is about 333. A new chip doubles peak FLOP/s and keeps the same memory bandwidth. Roughly how much faster does this kernel run on it?
--- choice
About twice as fast, because the chip is twice as fast.
--- choice correct
About the same speed, because the kernel is memory-bound and the bandwidth did not change.
--- choice
About √2 times as fast, because only half the time was compute.
--- why
At intensity 40 the roofline gives 40 × 3 × 10¹² = 1.2 × 10¹⁴ FLOP/s, far below either peak. Its time is set by bytes / bandwidth, which did not change. More peak only helps kernels right of the ridge, and doubling peak moves the ridge further right.

+++ question | Doubling the batch
--- ask
A multiply of m tokens by an 8192 × 8192 weight, in 16-bit numbers, takes time T on A-1 with m = 64. About how long does it take with m = 128?
--- choice correct
About T: the weight dominates the traffic, so the bytes barely grow, and the multiply is still memory-bound.
--- choice
About 2T, because there are twice as many FLOPs.
--- choice
About T/2, because the intensity doubles.
--- why
With m much smaller than d, bytes ≈ 2·d², the weight read once, and intensity ≈ m. At m = 64 or 128 the intensity is far left of the ridge at 333, so the time is about bytes / bandwidth, which hardly changes: the extra tokens ride along almost free. The time only starts growing with m once m passes the ridge.

+++ practice | The three kernels of an MLP
--- task
A transformer's MLP, for `tokens` tokens of width `hidden`, runs three kernels:

- `"up"`: a multiply of a tokens × hidden matrix by a hidden × (4·hidden) weight;
- `"gelu"`: GELU on every entry of the tokens × (4·hidden) result, assumed to cost 8 FLOPs per entry, reading each entry once and writing it once;
- `"down"`: a multiply of a tokens × (4·hidden) matrix by a (4·hidden) × hidden weight.

Write `mlp_report(accel, tokens, hidden, bytes_per)` that returns a list of three tuples, in that order: `(name, bound, microseconds)`, where `bound` is what `accel.bound` says and `microseconds` is `accel.time` times 10⁶, rounded to 3 decimal places with `round(..., 3)`. The starter has the finished cost model from the lesson.

For example, `mlp_report(ACCEL, 4096, 4096, 2)` starts with `("up", "compute", 549.756)`.
--- starter
class Accelerator:
    def __init__(self, name, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.name = name
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes

    def ridge(self):
        return self.peak_flops / self.mem_bw

    def attainable(self, intensity):
        return min(self.peak_flops, intensity * self.mem_bw)

    def time(self, flops, nbytes):
        return max(flops / self.peak_flops, nbytes / self.mem_bw)

    def bound(self, flops, nbytes):
        if flops / self.peak_flops >= nbytes / self.mem_bw:
            return "compute"
        return "memory"


def matmul_flops(m, k, n):
    return 2 * m * k * n


def matmul_bytes(m, k, n, bytes_per):
    return bytes_per * (m * k + k * n + m * n)


ACCEL = Accelerator("A-1", 1.0e15, 3.0e12, 80e9)


def mlp_report(accel, tokens, hidden, bytes_per):
    return []
--- solution
class Accelerator:
    def __init__(self, name, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.name = name
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes

    def ridge(self):
        return self.peak_flops / self.mem_bw

    def attainable(self, intensity):
        return min(self.peak_flops, intensity * self.mem_bw)

    def time(self, flops, nbytes):
        return max(flops / self.peak_flops, nbytes / self.mem_bw)

    def bound(self, flops, nbytes):
        if flops / self.peak_flops >= nbytes / self.mem_bw:
            return "compute"
        return "memory"


def matmul_flops(m, k, n):
    return 2 * m * k * n


def matmul_bytes(m, k, n, bytes_per):
    return bytes_per * (m * k + k * n + m * n)


ACCEL = Accelerator("A-1", 1.0e15, 3.0e12, 80e9)


def mlp_report(accel, tokens, hidden, bytes_per):
    wide = 4 * hidden
    entries = tokens * wide
    kernels = [
        ("up", matmul_flops(tokens, hidden, wide), matmul_bytes(tokens, hidden, wide, bytes_per)),
        ("gelu", 8 * entries, 2 * entries * bytes_per),
        ("down", matmul_flops(tokens, wide, hidden), matmul_bytes(tokens, wide, hidden, bytes_per)),
    ]
    return [(name, accel.bound(f, b), round(accel.time(f, b) * 1e6, 3)) for name, f, b in kernels]
--- hint
Work out (FLOPs, bytes) for each kernel first. The two multiplies use `matmul_flops` and `matmul_bytes` with the shapes in the task; GELU has tokens · 4 · hidden entries, 8 FLOPs each, and moves each entry twice (read and write).
--- hint
Then ask the accelerator: `accel.bound(f, b)` and `accel.time(f, b) * 1e6`, rounded to 3 places.
--- check case | Training-sized: the multiplies are compute-bound, GELU is not
mlp_report(ACCEL, 4096, 4096, 2)
=> [("up", "compute", 549.756), ("gelu", "memory", 89.478), ("down", "compute", 549.756)]
--- check case | Eight tokens: everything is memory-bound
mlp_report(ACCEL, 8, 4096, 2)
=> [("up", "memory", 44.848), ("gelu", "memory", 0.175), ("down", "memory", 44.848)]
--- check case | A tiny accelerator with whole-number speeds
mlp_report(Accelerator("toy", 100, 10, 1), 2, 2, 1)
=> [("up", "memory", 3600000.0), ("gelu", "memory", 3200000.0), ("down", "memory", 3600000.0)]
--- check test | 32-bit numbers make GELU twice as slow
mlp_report(ACCEL, 4096, 4096, 4)[1][2] == 178.957

+++ practice | How many tokens reach the ridge?
--- task
A linear layer multiplies an m × `d_in` matrix of activations by a `d_in` × `d_out` weight. Small m is memory-bound; large m is compute-bound.

Write `min_tokens(accel, d_in, d_out, bytes_per)` that returns the **smallest whole number m ≥ 1** for which `accel.bound(...)` of that multiply would say `"compute"`: compute time at least memory time. If no m ever gets there, return `None`.

Do it with exact arithmetic, not by trying m = 1, 2, 3, …: compute-bound means 2·m·d_in·d_out / peak ≥ bytes_per·(m·d_in + d_in·d_out + m·d_out) / bw. Multiply out and collect the m terms. Use `Fraction(accel.peak_flops)` and `Fraction(accel.mem_bw)` so nothing rounds (both are whole numbers stored as floats, which `Fraction` converts exactly).

For example, `min_tokens(ACCEL, 4096, 4096, 2)` is `399`.
--- starter
import math
from fractions import Fraction


class Accelerator:
    def __init__(self, name, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.name = name
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


ACCEL = Accelerator("A-1", 1.0e15, 3.0e12, 80e9)


def min_tokens(accel, d_in, d_out, bytes_per):
    return 1
--- solution
import math
from fractions import Fraction


class Accelerator:
    def __init__(self, name, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.name = name
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes


ACCEL = Accelerator("A-1", 1.0e15, 3.0e12, 80e9)


def min_tokens(accel, d_in, d_out, bytes_per):
    peak = Fraction(accel.peak_flops)
    bw = Fraction(accel.mem_bw)
    # m * (2*d_in*d_out*bw - peak*bytes_per*(d_in + d_out)) >= peak*bytes_per*d_in*d_out
    per_token = 2 * d_in * d_out * bw - peak * bytes_per * (d_in + d_out)
    if per_token <= 0:
        return None
    return max(1, math.ceil(peak * bytes_per * d_in * d_out / per_token))
--- hint
Multiply both sides by peak · bw. The inequality becomes m · (2·d_in·d_out·bw − peak·bytes_per·(d_in + d_out)) ≥ peak·bytes_per·d_in·d_out.
--- hint
If the bracket on the left is 0 or negative, more tokens never help: return `None`. Otherwise m is the ceiling of the right side divided by the bracket, and at least 1.
--- check case | The example: about the ridge, plus a little
min_tokens(ACCEL, 4096, 4096, 2)
=> 399
--- check case | Wider layers get closer to the ridge of 333
(min_tokens(ACCEL, 8192, 8192, 2), min_tokens(ACCEL, 8192, 28672, 2))
=> (363, 352)
--- check case | 32-bit numbers need far more tokens
min_tokens(ACCEL, 4096, 4096, 4)
=> 989
--- check case | A narrow layer can never be compute-bound
(min_tokens(ACCEL, 512, 512, 2), min_tokens(ACCEL, 300, 300, 2))
=> (None, None)
?? With d = 512 even an infinite batch has intensity below 333: the output and input traffic grow as fast as the FLOPs.
--- check case | Wide enough, barely
min_tokens(ACCEL, 1024, 1024, 2)
=> 956
--- check case | A toy accelerator whose ridge is 10
min_tokens(Accelerator("toy", 100, 10, 1), 10, 10, 1)
=> None

+++ practice | Tiled matrix multiply, with its memory traffic counted
--- task
The lesson counted the *least* traffic of a multiply: each number read once. A loop that reads A[i][p] and B[p][j] from main memory for every multiply-add reads 2·m·k·n numbers instead. **Tiling** gets close to the minimum: copy a t × t block of A and a t × t block of B into fast on-chip memory, do every multiply-add they allow, and move on.

Write `tiled_matmul(A, B, t)` for A of shape m × k and B of shape k × n (lists of lists). For each t × t output tile, starting at row `i0` and column `j0` (both stepping by t), keep a running tile of sums; for each block of the inner dimension starting at `p0` (stepping by t), **load** the A block (rows i0…i0+t−1, columns p0…p0+t−1) and the B block (rows p0…, columns j0…), add their product into the running tile, and when the inner loop is done, **store** the tile into C. Blocks at the edges are cut short where the matrix ends; count only the numbers that exist.

Return `(C, loads, stores)`: the product, the count of numbers loaded, and the count stored.

Then write `max_tile(sram_bytes, bytes_per)`: the largest whole t such that three t × t tiles (A block, B block and the running sums) fit in `sram_bytes`, that is 3·t²·bytes_per ≤ sram_bytes.

When t divides m, k and n, loads come to 2·m·k·n / t: tiles of side t cut traffic by t.
--- starter
import math


def tiled_matmul(A, B, t):
    m, k, n = len(A), len(B), len(B[0])
    C = [[sum(A[i][p] * B[p][j] for p in range(k)) for j in range(n)] for i in range(m)]
    return C, 0, 0


def max_tile(sram_bytes, bytes_per):
    return 0
--- solution
import math


def tiled_matmul(A, B, t):
    m, k, n = len(A), len(B), len(B[0])
    C = [[0] * n for _ in range(m)]
    loads = stores = 0
    for i0 in range(0, m, t):
        rows = range(i0, min(i0 + t, m))
        for j0 in range(0, n, t):
            cols = range(j0, min(j0 + t, n))
            acc = [[0] * len(cols) for _ in rows]
            for p0 in range(0, k, t):
                inner = range(p0, min(p0 + t, k))
                a = [[A[i][p] for p in inner] for i in rows]
                b = [[B[p][j] for j in cols] for p in inner]
                loads += len(rows) * len(inner) + len(inner) * len(cols)
                for x in range(len(rows)):
                    for y in range(len(cols)):
                        acc[x][y] += sum(a[x][z] * b[z][y] for z in range(len(inner)))
            for x, i in enumerate(rows):
                for y, j in enumerate(cols):
                    C[i][j] = acc[x][y]
            stores += len(rows) * len(cols)
    return C, loads, stores


def max_tile(sram_bytes, bytes_per):
    return math.isqrt(sram_bytes // (3 * bytes_per))
--- hint
Use `range(i0, min(i0 + t, m))` for the rows of a tile, and the same pattern for columns and the inner dimension, so edge tiles are cut short. Loading a block adds (its rows × its columns) to `loads`.
--- hint
The running tile starts at zeros for each (i0, j0) and is stored once, after the p0 loop: add its size to `stores` then.
--- hint
For `max_tile`, t² ≤ sram_bytes / (3·bytes_per). `math.isqrt` of the whole-number quotient gives the largest such t.
--- check case | A 5 x 3 times 3 x 4 product with t = 2
tiled_matmul([[i * 3 + j for j in range(3)] for i in range(5)], [[i - j for j in range(4)] for i in range(3)], 2)
=> ([[5, 2, -1, -4], [14, 2, -10, -22], [23, 2, -19, -40], [32, 2, -28, -58], [41, 2, -37, -76]], 66, 20)
--- check case | t = 1 is the naive loop: 2mkn loads
tiled_matmul([[i * 3 + j for j in range(3)] for i in range(5)], [[i - j for j in range(4)] for i in range(3)], 1)[1:]
=> (120, 20)
--- check case | A tile bigger than the matrix loads each number once
tiled_matmul([[i * 3 + j for j in range(3)] for i in range(5)], [[i - j for j in range(4)] for i in range(3)], 8)[1:]
=> (27, 20)
--- check test | Traffic falls as 2mkn / t, and the product stays right
(lambda r: (lambda A, B: all(tiled_matmul(A, B, t)[1] == 2 * 8 * 16 * 12 // t and tiled_matmul(A, B, t)[0] == [[sum(A[i][p] * B[p][j] for p in range(16)) for j in range(12)] for i in range(8)] for t in (1, 2, 4)))([[r.randint(-3, 3) for _ in range(16)] for _ in range(8)], [[r.randint(-3, 3) for _ in range(12)] for _ in range(16)]))(__import__("random").Random(1))
--- check case | Tiles that fit in on-chip memory
(max_tile(262144, 2), max_tile(262144, 4), max_tile(229376, 2))
=> (209, 147, 195)
--- check case | Too little memory for even one number per tile
(max_tile(5, 2), max_tile(6, 2))
=> (0, 1)

+++ practice | Padding: shapes that waste the machine
--- task
Matrix units do their work in fixed blocks. Assume a unit that only multiplies whole `tile` × `tile` blocks: a dimension that is not a multiple of `tile` is padded up to the next multiple with zeros, and the zeros cost FLOPs like any other number.

Write `pad_to(d, multiple)`, the smallest multiple of `multiple` that is at least `d` (for d ≥ 1), and `padded_utilization(m, k, n, tile)`: the fraction of the FLOPs spent on real numbers, m·k·n divided by the product of the three padded dimensions, as a float.

For example, `pad_to(50257, 64)` is `50304` (a real vocabulary size, often padded exactly this way), and `padded_utilization(129, 128, 128, 128)` is about 0.504: one row too many doubles the work.
--- starter
def pad_to(d, multiple):
    return d


def padded_utilization(m, k, n, tile):
    return 1.0
--- solution
def pad_to(d, multiple):
    return -(-d // multiple) * multiple


def padded_utilization(m, k, n, tile):
    padded = pad_to(m, tile) * pad_to(k, tile) * pad_to(n, tile)
    return m * k * n / padded
--- hint
Rounding up a whole-number division can be written `-(-d // multiple)`. Multiply it back by `multiple`.
--- hint
Pad each of m, k and n on its own, then divide the real product by the padded one.
--- check case | Padding a vocabulary
(pad_to(50257, 64), pad_to(50257, 128), pad_to(128, 128), pad_to(1, 128))
=> (50304, 50304, 128, 128)
--- check case | Multiples of the tile waste nothing
round(padded_utilization(4096, 4096, 4096, 128), 4)
=> 1.0
--- check case | One row over the tile nearly halves the useful work
round(padded_utilization(129, 128, 128, 128), 4)
=> 0.5039
--- check case | A matrix smaller than one tile in every direction
round(padded_utilization(100, 100, 100, 128), 4)
=> 0.4768
--- check case | A big odd vocabulary loses little to padding
(round(padded_utilization(4096, 4096, 50257, 128), 4), round(padded_utilization(4096, 4096, 50304, 128), 4))
=> (0.9991, 1.0)

+++ practice | Debug: the 32-bit kernel that looks compute-bound
--- task
`report(accel, kernels)` takes kernels as `(name, flops, elements, bytes_per)`: the FLOPs, the number of elements read and written in total, and the bytes per element. It should return `(name, bound, intensity)` for each, where the intensity is in **FLOPs per byte**, rounded to 2 places, and the bound is `"compute"` if the intensity is at least the ridge, otherwise `"memory"`.

It says an attention kernel in 32-bit numbers, `("attn", 6e11, 1e9, 4)`, is compute-bound with intensity 600.0. It does 600 FLOPs per *element*, but each element is 4 bytes, so its intensity is 150.0 FLOPs per byte and it is memory-bound on A-1. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
class Accelerator:
    def __init__(self, name, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.name = name
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes

    def ridge(self):
        return self.peak_flops / self.mem_bw


ACCEL = Accelerator("A-1", 1.0e15, 3.0e12, 80e9)


def report(accel, kernels):
    out = []
    for name, flops, elements, bytes_per in kernels:
        intensity = flops / elements
        bound = "compute" if intensity >= accel.ridge() else "memory"
        out.append((name, bound, round(intensity, 2)))
    return out
--- solution
class Accelerator:
    def __init__(self, name, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.name = name
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes

    def ridge(self):
        return self.peak_flops / self.mem_bw


ACCEL = Accelerator("A-1", 1.0e15, 3.0e12, 80e9)


def report(accel, kernels):
    out = []
    for name, flops, elements, bytes_per in kernels:
        intensity = flops / (elements * bytes_per)
        bound = "compute" if intensity >= accel.ridge() else "memory"
        out.append((name, bound, round(intensity, 2)))
    return out
--- hint
The ridge is in FLOPs per byte. What unit is `flops / elements` in?
--- hint
The bytes moved are `elements * bytes_per`. Divide by that.
--- check case | The kernel from the task
report(ACCEL, [("attn", 6e11, 1e9, 4)])
=> [("attn", "memory", 150.0)]
--- check case | A mix of kernels
report(ACCEL, [("attn", 6e11, 1e9, 4), ("ln", 5e9, 1e9, 2), ("mm", 2e12, 1e9, 2)])
=> [("attn", "memory", 150.0), ("ln", "memory", 2.5), ("mm", "compute", 1000.0)]
--- check case | One-byte numbers
report(ACCEL, [("fp8 mm", 4e11, 1e9, 1)])
=> [("fp8 mm", "compute", 400.0)]
--- check case | No kernels
report(ACCEL, [])
=> []

+++ practice | Fusing a chain of elementwise kernels
--- task
After a multiply, a model often runs several elementwise kernels in a row on the same tensor of `n` numbers: add a bias, apply GELU, scale, add a residual. Run separately, each kernel reads the tensor and writes it back: 2·n·bytes_per bytes each. **Fused** into one kernel, the chain reads once and writes once: 2·n·bytes_per bytes in all, while the FLOPs add up.

Write `fusion_gain(accel, n, flops_per_op, bytes_per)`. `flops_per_op` is a list with the FLOPs per number of each kernel in the chain. Use `accel.time` for every kernel. Return a tuple `(unfused_us, fused_us, speedup)`: the total time of the separate kernels and the time of the fused one in microseconds, each rounded to 3 places, and unfused / fused rounded to 3 places (compute the speedup from the unrounded times).

For example, `fusion_gain(ACCEL, 10**8, [1, 4, 2, 1], 2)` is `(533.333, 133.333, 4.0)`.
--- starter
class Accelerator:
    def __init__(self, name, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.name = name
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes

    def time(self, flops, nbytes):
        return max(flops / self.peak_flops, nbytes / self.mem_bw)


ACCEL = Accelerator("A-1", 1.0e15, 3.0e12, 80e9)


def fusion_gain(accel, n, flops_per_op, bytes_per):
    return (0.0, 0.0, 1.0)
--- solution
class Accelerator:
    def __init__(self, name, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.name = name
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes

    def time(self, flops, nbytes):
        return max(flops / self.peak_flops, nbytes / self.mem_bw)


ACCEL = Accelerator("A-1", 1.0e15, 3.0e12, 80e9)


def fusion_gain(accel, n, flops_per_op, bytes_per):
    traffic = 2 * n * bytes_per
    unfused = sum(accel.time(n * f, traffic) for f in flops_per_op)
    fused = accel.time(n * sum(flops_per_op), traffic)
    return (round(unfused * 1e6, 3), round(fused * 1e6, 3), round(unfused / fused, 3))
--- hint
Each separate kernel costs `accel.time(n * f, 2 * n * bytes_per)`. The fused kernel costs `accel.time(n * total_f, 2 * n * bytes_per)` with the FLOPs added up.
--- hint
While every kernel is memory-bound, the speedup is the number of kernels. When the fused kernel becomes compute-bound, it is less.
--- check case | Four light kernels: fusing saves three passes over memory
fusion_gain(ACCEL, 10**8, [1, 4, 2, 1], 2)
=> (533.333, 133.333, 4.0)
--- check case | One kernel: nothing to fuse
fusion_gain(ACCEL, 10**8, [3], 2)
=> (133.333, 133.333, 1.0)
--- check case | Heavy kernels: the fused one still waits on memory
fusion_gain(ACCEL, 10**8, [200, 200, 200], 2)
=> (400.0, 133.333, 3.0)
--- check case | Ten kernels on 32-bit numbers
fusion_gain(ACCEL, 2**20, [1] * 10, 4)
=> (27.962, 2.796, 10.0)
--- check case | So heavy that fusing makes it compute-bound
fusion_gain(ACCEL, 10**6, [2000, 2000], 2)
=> (4.0, 4.0, 1.0)

=== mlsys1-02 | Numerics: fp32, bf16, fp16 and fp8, overflow, loss scaling and accumulation
--- teach
Last lesson's numbers all assumed 16-bit numbers: 2 bytes each, and the accelerator's peak quoted for them. That choice halves memory and traffic and doubles the speed of the matrix units compared with 32-bit floats. It is also where training runs quietly go wrong: a gradient that rounds to zero, a sum that stops growing, a loss that turns into `inf`. This lesson simulates the formats bit by bit, so you can see exactly where each one breaks, and builds the two tools that make low precision safe: **loss scaling** and **wide accumulation**.

### The fields, again

In Computer Organization you decoded IEEE-754 floats by hand. Every format here has the same three fields:

- a **sign** bit;
- an **exponent** of e bits, stored with a **bias** of 2^(e−1) − 1, which sets the **range**: how big and how small a number can be;
- a **mantissa** (the fraction) of m bits, which sets the **precision**: how many numbers there are between two powers of two.

A normal number is 1.fraction × 2^exponent. The all-zeros exponent holds zero and the **subnormals**, which give up the leading 1 to reach closer to zero. In the IEEE formats the all-ones exponent holds infinity and NaN.

### Five formats, one rule

| format | exponent bits | mantissa bits | largest | smallest normal | gap after 1.0 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| fp32 | 8 | 23 | 3.40 × 10³⁸ | 1.18 × 10⁻³⁸ | 1.19 × 10⁻⁷ |
| bf16 | 8 | 7 | 3.39 × 10³⁸ | 1.18 × 10⁻³⁸ | 0.0078 |
| fp16 | 5 | 10 | 65,504 | 6.10 × 10⁻⁵ | 0.00098 |
| fp8 e5m2 | 5 | 2 | 57,344 | 6.10 × 10⁻⁵ | 0.25 |
| fp8 e4m3 | 4 | 3 | 448 | 0.0156 | 0.125 |

Read the table in two directions. Down the exponent column is **range**: **[[bf16|bf16]]** keeps fp32's 8 exponent bits, so it covers the same range with far less precision. fp16 spends its bits on precision instead, and pays with a top value of 65,504. Down the mantissa column is precision: the gap after 1.0, called **machine epsilon**, is 2^−m.

The general formulas, for a format with e exponent bits and m mantissa bits:

- the smallest normal number is 2^(1 − bias);
- the smallest subnormal is that times 2^−m;
- the largest IEEE number is (2 − 2^−m) × 2^bias.

The fp8 e4m3 format is the exception. It has **no infinity**: it uses the all-ones exponent for ordinary numbers and keeps only one pattern for NaN, so its largest number is (2 − 2^(1−m)) × 2^(bias+1) = 1.75 × 2⁸ = 448. Converting a too-big number to it **[[saturates|saturate]]**: it becomes ±448, not infinity. (That is the conversion mode training code uses, and this course's assumption.)

### Rounding to a format

Every result is rounded to the nearest number the format holds. On an exact tie it goes to the one whose last mantissa bit is 0: **[[round to nearest, ties to even|round-even]]**.

The spacing between neighbouring numbers is constant inside one power of two. Between 2^E and 2^(E+1), a format with m mantissa bits has numbers every 2^(E − m). So rounding x is: find the power of two it falls in, divide by the spacing, round to a whole number, multiply back. Here is 0.1 into bf16 by hand:

```python
import math

frac, power = math.frexp(0.1)   # 0.1 == frac * 2**power, with 0.5 <= frac < 1
print(frac, power)              # 0.8 -3, so 0.1 = 1.6 * 2**-4
gap = 2.0 ** (-4 - 7)           # bf16 keeps 7 bits after the leading 1
print(0.1 / gap)                # 204.8 steps of the gap
print(205 * gap)                # 0.10009765625, the nearest bf16 number
```

Python's built-in `round` already rounds ties to even: `round(2.5)` is 2 and `round(3.5)` is 4.

Two places need care. **Below the smallest normal**, the spacing stops shrinking: it stays at the smallest subnormal, which is why tiny numbers lose precision gradually and then become 0. **Above the largest number**, the result overflows: to infinity in the IEEE formats, to ±448 in e4m3. The tie rule decides the boundary: in fp16, 65,519 rounds down to 65,504, but 65,520 is exactly halfway to 65,536 and ties to the even side, which is past the top, so it becomes `inf`.

### Overflow and underflow in training

**Overflow** turns a value into `inf`, and `inf − inf` or `0 × inf` makes NaN, which spreads through every sum it touches. In fp16, activations or attention scores above 65,504 do this. bf16 almost never overflows: its range is fp32's.

**Underflow** is quieter. Gradients of a large model are often around 10⁻⁶ to 10⁻⁹. In fp16, everything below about 3 × 10⁻⁸ (half the smallest subnormal) becomes exactly 0, and below 6 × 10⁻⁵ precision is already being lost. Those weights stop learning, and nothing reports an error.

### Loss scaling

The fix for fp16 is **loss scaling**. Multiply the loss by a big constant S, say 2¹⁰ = 1,024, before the backward pass. By the chain rule every gradient is then multiplied by S too, which lifts it out of the underflow zone. Before the optimizer uses the gradients, divide them by S again, in fp32. Choosing [[S as a power of two|loss-scale]] means scaling only changes the exponent, so it adds no rounding error at all.

S cannot be too big, or the largest gradients overflow. Real systems choose it **dynamically**: if any gradient comes out `inf` or NaN, skip this step and halve S; after a run of good steps (often 1,000 or 2,000), double S. The scale hovers right below the overflow line. bf16 needs none of this, which is the main reason it became the default for training.

### Accumulation precision

Adding many numbers in a narrow format fails in a way that surprises people. In fp16, the gap between numbers near 2,048 is 2. So 2,048 + 1 is exactly halfway between 2,048 and 2,050, ties to even, and stays **2,048**. A running sum of ones stops growing at 2,048 forever. In bf16 it stops at 256.

A dot product of length 4,096, the inner loop of a matrix multiply, is exactly such a sum. So matrix units multiply 16-bit (or 8-bit) inputs but **[[accumulate in fp32|accumulate]]**, and the optimizer keeps an fp32 **master copy** of the weights: an update of 10⁻⁵ to a weight of 1.0 is below bf16's gap of 0.0078 and would vanish.

### Mixed precision, the standard recipe

1. Keep fp32 master weights and fp32 optimizer state.
2. Each step, cast the weights to bf16 (or fp16) and run forward and backward in 16-bit, with fp32 accumulation inside every matrix multiply.
3. For fp16 only: scale the loss, check the gradients for inf and NaN, unscale.
4. Update the fp32 master weights with the gradients.

**FP8** goes further for the matrix multiplies of the largest runs: e4m3 for weights and activations (more precision), e5m2 for gradients (more range). Its range is so narrow that every tensor gets its own **scale**: multiply it so its largest absolute value, its **amax**, lands near the format's top, convert, and divide the scale out after the multiply.

**Watch out:**

- **"16-bit" is two formats.** fp16 and bf16 are not interchangeable: code that is safe in bf16 can overflow in fp16, and a model trained in bf16 can produce `inf` when served in fp16.
- **Skipped steps are not free.** A dynamic loss scaler that skips steps often, or a scale stuck at 1, means something overflows every step; the cause is usually a real numerical problem, not the scaler.
- **Summing in low precision.** Any long reduction (a loss averaged over a batch, a norm, a softmax denominator) must be accumulated in fp32, even if its inputs are 16-bit.

::: context bf16 A format designed for training
bfloat16, "brain floating point", was designed at Google Brain for training neural networks on its TPU accelerators. The idea was that networks care much more about range than precision: a gradient of 10⁻²⁰ must not vanish, but a weight off by 0.4% does little harm. So bf16 is exactly the top 16 bits of an fp32 number: the same sign and 8-bit exponent, and a mantissa cut from 23 bits to 7. Converting fp32 to bf16 is a rounding of the low 16 bits, which is cheap in hardware. Most accelerators designed since support it, and it is now the default training format.
:::

::: context saturate Why e4m3 has no infinity
With only 8 bits there are 256 patterns, and the IEEE layout would spend 16 of them on infinities and NaNs. The e4m3 format agreed by several chip makers (the "OCP 8-bit floating point" specification) instead keeps only the patterns with all exponent and mantissa bits set as NaN and uses the rest of the top exponent for ordinary numbers. That doubles its range from 240 to 448. Without infinity, an overflowing value has to go somewhere: conversion either produces NaN or saturates to the largest number, and training libraries choose saturation, so one outlier is clipped rather than poisoning the whole tensor.
:::

::: context round-even Why ties go to even
Rounding every tie upwards (the school rule, 2.5 to 3) biases a long computation: on average results drift up by a quarter of the spacing every time a tie occurs. Rounding ties to the even neighbour sends half the ties up and half down, so the errors cancel on average. IEEE-754 made it the default for that reason. It is also why the sum of ones stalls exactly at 2,048 in fp16: 2,049 is a tie between 2,048 (even last bit) and 2,050 (odd), and the tie goes down.
:::

::: context loss-scale Why the scale is a power of two
Multiplying a float by 2^k only adds k to its exponent; the mantissa bits are untouched, so the product is exact unless it overflows or underflows. That makes scaling and unscaling by a power of two lossless, while scaling by, say, 1,000 would round every gradient twice. Dynamic loss scalers in real frameworks start around 2¹⁶ = 65,536, halve on any inf or NaN, and double after a fixed number of good steps, so the scale settles right under the largest value that does not overflow.
:::

::: context accumulate Where the fp32 accumulators live
A matrix unit takes small tiles of 16-bit or 8-bit inputs, multiplies them, and adds the products into accumulator registers that are 32 bits wide. Only when the whole dot product is finished is the result rounded back to 16 bits for storage. The extra width costs little silicon compared with the multipliers, and it is the difference between a matrix multiply that is accurate to the format and one whose error grows with the length of the dot product. Some fp8 designs accumulate in narrower registers and must promote partial sums to fp32 every so often to keep long sums accurate.
:::
--- task
Write the course's number-format simulator. A format is a tuple `(exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf)`; the starter defines `FP32`, `BF16`, `FP16`, `E5M2` and `E4M3` (the only one with `has_inf` False). Write:

- `largest(fmt)`: the largest finite number. For IEEE formats (`has_inf` True) it is (2 − 2^−m) × 2^bias; for formats without infinity it is (2 − 2^(1−m)) × 2^(bias+1). The bias is 2^(e−1) − 1.
- `smallest_normal(fmt)`: 2^(1 − bias).
- `smallest_subnormal(fmt)`: the smallest normal times 2^−m.
- `epsilon(fmt)`: the gap between 1.0 and the next number, 2^−m.
- `round_to(x, fmt)`: the number of the format nearest to the Python float `x`, as a float, rounding ties to even. Keep the sign of `x`, including for results that round to zero (`round_to(-1e-9, FP16)` is `-0.0`). Zero and NaN come back unchanged. A result bigger than `largest(fmt)` becomes `inf` (with the sign of `x`) when `has_inf` is True, and ±`largest(fmt)` when it is False. An infinite `x` stays infinite in an IEEE format and saturates in the others.

Below the smallest normal, the spacing between numbers stays at the smallest subnormal. For example, `round_to(0.1, BF16)` is `0.10009765625`, `round_to(65520.0, FP16)` is `inf`, and `round_to(500.0, E4M3)` is `448.0`.
--- starter
import math

FP32 = (8, 23, True)
BF16 = (8, 7, True)
FP16 = (5, 10, True)
E5M2 = (5, 2, True)
E4M3 = (4, 3, False)   # no infinity: overflow saturates to the largest number


def largest(fmt):
    return 0.0


def smallest_normal(fmt):
    return 0.0


def smallest_subnormal(fmt):
    return 0.0


def epsilon(fmt):
    return 0.0


def round_to(x, fmt):
    return x
--- solution
import math

FP32 = (8, 23, True)
BF16 = (8, 7, True)
FP16 = (5, 10, True)
E5M2 = (5, 2, True)
E4M3 = (4, 3, False)   # no infinity: overflow saturates to the largest number


def bias(fmt):
    return 2 ** (fmt[0] - 1) - 1


def largest(fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if has_inf:
        return (2 - 2.0 ** -man_bits) * 2.0 ** bias(fmt)
    return (2 - 2.0 ** (1 - man_bits)) * 2.0 ** (bias(fmt) + 1)


def smallest_normal(fmt):
    return 2.0 ** (1 - bias(fmt))


def smallest_subnormal(fmt):
    return smallest_normal(fmt) * 2.0 ** -fmt[1]


def epsilon(fmt):
    return 2.0 ** -fmt[1]


def round_to(x, fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if math.isnan(x) or x == 0:
        return x
    top = largest(fmt)
    if math.isinf(x):
        return x if has_inf else math.copysign(top, x)
    a = abs(x)
    # The power of two that a falls in, but never below the smallest normal's.
    power = max(math.frexp(a)[1] - 1, 1 - bias(fmt))
    spacing = 2.0 ** (power - man_bits)
    q = round(a / spacing) * spacing
    if q > top:
        q = math.inf if has_inf else top
    return math.copysign(q, x)
--- hint
Write a small `bias(fmt)` helper first; the four properties are then one formula each from the task.
--- hint
For `round_to`, handle NaN, zero and infinity first. For the rest, work on `abs(x)`: `math.frexp(a)[1] - 1` is the power of two it falls in. Clamp that power to at least 1 − bias, so subnormals use the smallest subnormal as their spacing.
--- hint
The spacing is 2 to the power (power − man_bits). `round(a / spacing) * spacing` rounds ties to even. Then check for overflow against `largest(fmt)`, and put the sign back with `math.copysign`, which also gives -0.0.
