--- check test | Rounding to fp32 matches the machine's own 32-bit rounding
(lambda st, r: all(round_to(v, FP32) == st.unpack("<f", st.pack("<f", v))[0] for v in [r.uniform(-1e6, 1e6) * 10 ** r.randint(-30, 30) for _ in range(3000)]))(__import__("struct"), __import__("random").Random(5))
?? Python's struct module stores a float in 32 bits with IEEE rounding, so your fp32 results should match it exactly.

+++ question | Why bf16 needs no loss scaling
--- ask
Training in fp16 needs loss scaling; training in bf16 almost never does. Why?
--- choice
bf16 has more mantissa bits, so small gradients keep their precision.
--- choice correct
bf16 has fp32's 8 exponent bits, so its smallest normal number is about 10⁻³⁸ and small gradients do not underflow.
--- choice
bf16 hardware rounds tiny numbers up instead of to zero.
--- why
Underflow is a range problem, and range comes from the exponent. bf16 has the same 8-bit exponent as fp32, so gradients of 10⁻⁹ are ordinary normal numbers in it. It actually has fewer mantissa bits than fp16 (7 against 10), which is why it needs fp32 master weights and fp32 accumulation, but not loss scaling.

+++ question | A sum that stops
--- ask
A loss is computed as the sum of 10,000 per-token losses, each about 1.0, with the running total kept in fp16. About what total does it report?
--- choice
10,000, because each loss is exactly representable.
--- choice correct
About 2,048, because above 2,048 the gap between fp16 numbers is 2, so adding 1.0 rounds back to the same total.
--- choice
65,504, the largest fp16 number.
--- choice
`inf`, because the sum overflows.
--- why
Between 2,048 and 4,096, fp16 numbers are 2 apart. 2,048 + 1 = 2,049 is a tie between 2,048 and 2,050 and ties to even, which is 2,048, so the total never moves again. That is why every reduction is accumulated in fp32.

+++ practice | A running sum in a narrow format
--- task
Write `accumulate(values, fmt)` that adds up a list of floats the way hardware with a narrow accumulator would: start from 0.0, round each value to `fmt`, and after every addition round the running total to `fmt` again. Return the final total. The starter has `round_to` and the formats from the lesson.

For example, `accumulate([1.0] * 4096, FP16)` is `2048.0`, while in `FP32` it is `4096.0`. An empty list sums to `0.0`. Once a total overflows to `inf` it stays `inf` (adding a negative number to `inf` does not bring it back).
--- starter
import math

FP32 = (8, 23, True)
BF16 = (8, 7, True)
FP16 = (5, 10, True)
E5M2 = (5, 2, True)
E4M3 = (4, 3, False)


def bias(fmt):
    return 2 ** (fmt[0] - 1) - 1


def largest(fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if has_inf:
        return (2 - 2.0 ** -man_bits) * 2.0 ** bias(fmt)
    return (2 - 2.0 ** (1 - man_bits)) * 2.0 ** (bias(fmt) + 1)


def round_to(x, fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if math.isnan(x) or x == 0:
        return x
    top = largest(fmt)
    if math.isinf(x):
        return x if has_inf else math.copysign(top, x)
    a = abs(x)
    power = max(math.frexp(a)[1] - 1, 1 - bias(fmt))
    spacing = 2.0 ** (power - man_bits)
    q = round(a / spacing) * spacing
    if q > top:
        q = math.inf if has_inf else top
    return math.copysign(q, x)


def accumulate(values, fmt):
    return sum(values)
--- solution
import math

FP32 = (8, 23, True)
BF16 = (8, 7, True)
FP16 = (5, 10, True)
E5M2 = (5, 2, True)
E4M3 = (4, 3, False)


def bias(fmt):
    return 2 ** (fmt[0] - 1) - 1


def largest(fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if has_inf:
        return (2 - 2.0 ** -man_bits) * 2.0 ** bias(fmt)
    return (2 - 2.0 ** (1 - man_bits)) * 2.0 ** (bias(fmt) + 1)


def round_to(x, fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if math.isnan(x) or x == 0:
        return x
    top = largest(fmt)
    if math.isinf(x):
        return x if has_inf else math.copysign(top, x)
    a = abs(x)
    power = max(math.frexp(a)[1] - 1, 1 - bias(fmt))
    spacing = 2.0 ** (power - man_bits)
    q = round(a / spacing) * spacing
    if q > top:
        q = math.inf if has_inf else top
    return math.copysign(q, x)


def accumulate(values, fmt):
    total = 0.0
    for v in values:
        total = round_to(total + round_to(v, fmt), fmt)
    return total
--- hint
Keep a running `total`. For each value, round the value to the format, add it, and round the new total to the format.
--- hint
`inf` plus any finite number is `inf` in Python too, so overflow takes care of itself once `round_to` returns `inf`.
--- check case | Ones stall at 2,048 in fp16 and at 256 in bf16
(accumulate([1.0] * 4096, FP16), accumulate([1.0] * 4096, BF16), accumulate([1.0] * 4096, FP32))
=> (2048.0, 256.0, 4096.0)
--- check case | A thousand hundredths
(accumulate([0.01] * 1000, FP16), accumulate([0.01] * 1000, BF16))
=> (9.953125, 4.0)
--- check case | Empty list
accumulate([], FP16)
=> 0.0
--- check case | Overflow is sticky
(accumulate([70000.0], FP16), accumulate([60000.0, 10000.0], FP16), accumulate([60000.0, 10000.0, -20000.0], FP16))
=> (float("inf"), float("inf"), float("inf"))
--- check case | fp8 accumulation is very coarse
accumulate([0.1] * 10, E4M3)
=> 1.125

+++ practice | Choosing a loss scale
--- task
A list of fp32 gradients is about to be cast to a 16-bit format. Write two functions (the starter has `round_to` and the formats):

- `underflow_fraction(grads, fmt, scale)`: of the **nonzero** gradients, the fraction that become exactly 0 when `g * scale` is rounded to `fmt`. Return `0.0` if there are no nonzero gradients.
- `best_scale(grads, fmt)`: the largest k from 0 to 24 such that **no** gradient becomes infinite when `g * 2**k` is rounded to `fmt`. Return `None` if even k = 0 overflows.

For example, with `grads = [1e-9, 2e-8, 2.5e-8, 2e-7, 5e-6, 1e-4, -1e-8, 0.0]`, `underflow_fraction(grads, FP16, 1)` is 4/7, about 0.5714, and with scale 2⁸ nothing underflows.
--- starter
import math

FP32 = (8, 23, True)
BF16 = (8, 7, True)
FP16 = (5, 10, True)


def bias(fmt):
    return 2 ** (fmt[0] - 1) - 1


def largest(fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if has_inf:
        return (2 - 2.0 ** -man_bits) * 2.0 ** bias(fmt)
    return (2 - 2.0 ** (1 - man_bits)) * 2.0 ** (bias(fmt) + 1)


def round_to(x, fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if math.isnan(x) or x == 0:
        return x
    top = largest(fmt)
    if math.isinf(x):
        return x if has_inf else math.copysign(top, x)
    a = abs(x)
    power = max(math.frexp(a)[1] - 1, 1 - bias(fmt))
    spacing = 2.0 ** (power - man_bits)
    q = round(a / spacing) * spacing
    if q > top:
        q = math.inf if has_inf else top
    return math.copysign(q, x)


def underflow_fraction(grads, fmt, scale):
    return 0.0


def best_scale(grads, fmt):
    return 0
--- solution
import math

FP32 = (8, 23, True)
BF16 = (8, 7, True)
FP16 = (5, 10, True)


def bias(fmt):
    return 2 ** (fmt[0] - 1) - 1


def largest(fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if has_inf:
        return (2 - 2.0 ** -man_bits) * 2.0 ** bias(fmt)
    return (2 - 2.0 ** (1 - man_bits)) * 2.0 ** (bias(fmt) + 1)


def round_to(x, fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if math.isnan(x) or x == 0:
        return x
    top = largest(fmt)
    if math.isinf(x):
        return x if has_inf else math.copysign(top, x)
    a = abs(x)
    power = max(math.frexp(a)[1] - 1, 1 - bias(fmt))
    spacing = 2.0 ** (power - man_bits)
    q = round(a / spacing) * spacing
    if q > top:
        q = math.inf if has_inf else top
    return math.copysign(q, x)


def underflow_fraction(grads, fmt, scale):
    nonzero = [g for g in grads if g != 0]
    if not nonzero:
        return 0.0
    lost = sum(1 for g in nonzero if round_to(g * scale, fmt) == 0)
    return lost / len(nonzero)


def best_scale(grads, fmt):
    best = None
    for k in range(25):
        if any(math.isinf(round_to(g * 2.0 ** k, fmt)) for g in grads):
            break
        best = k
    return best
--- hint
For the fraction, filter out the zeros first, then count the ones whose scaled, rounded value `== 0`.
--- hint
Overflow only gets worse as k grows, so try k = 0, 1, 2, … and stop at the first k where some gradient becomes `inf`; the answer is the k before it.
--- check case | Underflow falls as the scale grows
[round(underflow_fraction([1e-9, 2e-8, 2.5e-8, 2e-7, 5e-6, 1e-4, -1e-8, 0.0], FP16, s), 4) for s in (1, 2 ** 4, 2 ** 8, 2 ** 16)]
=> [0.5714, 0.1429, 0.0, 0.0]
--- check case | bf16 loses nothing, and no gradients means nothing lost
(underflow_fraction([1e-9, 2e-8, 2.5e-8, 2e-7, 5e-6, 1e-4, -1e-8, 0.0], BF16, 1), underflow_fraction([], FP16, 1), underflow_fraction([0.0, 0.0], FP16, 1))
=> (0.0, 0.0, 0.0)
--- check case | The largest safe power of two
(best_scale([1e-9, 2e-8, 2.5e-8, 2e-7, 5e-6, 1e-4, -1e-8, 0.0], FP16), best_scale([1.0, 2e-8], FP16), best_scale([3.0], FP16))
=> (24, 15, 14)
--- check case | Already overflowing at scale 1
best_scale([70000.0], FP16)
=> None
--- check case | Zeros never overflow
(best_scale([0.0], FP16), best_scale([], FP16))
=> (24, 24)

+++ practice | A dynamic loss scaler
--- task
Simulate the dynamic loss scaler from the lesson. `max_grads[i]` is the largest absolute fp32 gradient that step i would produce with a scale of 1 (it may be NaN when the step itself is broken). For each step, in order:

- if `round_to(g * scale, fmt)` is not finite (it is `inf` or NaN), the step is **skipped**: record its index, halve the scale, and reset the count of good steps to 0;
- otherwise the step is applied: add 1 to the count of good steps, and when the count reaches `growth_interval`, double the scale and reset the count to 0.

Write `run_scaler(max_grads, init_scale, growth_interval, fmt)` returning `(skipped, final_scale)`: the list of skipped step indexes and the scale after the last step. The starter has `round_to` and the formats.

For example, `run_scaler([0.01, 0.01, 3.0, 0.01], 2.0 ** 14, 2, FP16)` returns `([2], 16384.0)`: two good steps double the scale to 32,768, step 2 overflows (3 × 32,768 > 65,504) and halves it back to 16,384, and step 3 is good but one good step is not enough to grow again.
--- starter
import math

BF16 = (8, 7, True)
FP16 = (5, 10, True)


def bias(fmt):
    return 2 ** (fmt[0] - 1) - 1


def largest(fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if has_inf:
        return (2 - 2.0 ** -man_bits) * 2.0 ** bias(fmt)
    return (2 - 2.0 ** (1 - man_bits)) * 2.0 ** (bias(fmt) + 1)


def round_to(x, fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if math.isnan(x) or x == 0:
        return x
    top = largest(fmt)
    if math.isinf(x):
        return x if has_inf else math.copysign(top, x)
    a = abs(x)
    power = max(math.frexp(a)[1] - 1, 1 - bias(fmt))
    spacing = 2.0 ** (power - man_bits)
    q = round(a / spacing) * spacing
    if q > top:
        q = math.inf if has_inf else top
    return math.copysign(q, x)


def run_scaler(max_grads, init_scale, growth_interval, fmt):
    return [], init_scale
--- solution
import math

BF16 = (8, 7, True)
FP16 = (5, 10, True)


def bias(fmt):
    return 2 ** (fmt[0] - 1) - 1


def largest(fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if has_inf:
        return (2 - 2.0 ** -man_bits) * 2.0 ** bias(fmt)
    return (2 - 2.0 ** (1 - man_bits)) * 2.0 ** (bias(fmt) + 1)


def round_to(x, fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if math.isnan(x) or x == 0:
        return x
    top = largest(fmt)
    if math.isinf(x):
        return x if has_inf else math.copysign(top, x)
    a = abs(x)
    power = max(math.frexp(a)[1] - 1, 1 - bias(fmt))
    spacing = 2.0 ** (power - man_bits)
    q = round(a / spacing) * spacing
    if q > top:
        q = math.inf if has_inf else top
    return math.copysign(q, x)


def run_scaler(max_grads, init_scale, growth_interval, fmt):
    scale = init_scale
    good = 0
    skipped = []
    for step, g in enumerate(max_grads):
        if not math.isfinite(round_to(g * scale, fmt)):
            skipped.append(step)
            scale /= 2
            good = 0
        else:
            good += 1
            if good == growth_interval:
                scale *= 2
                good = 0
    return skipped, scale
--- hint
Keep three variables: `scale`, the count of good steps, and the list of skipped indexes. `math.isfinite` is False for both `inf` and NaN.
--- hint
On a skip, the good-step count goes back to 0, so growth has to start over.
--- check case | The example from the task
run_scaler([0.01, 0.01, 3.0, 0.01], 2.0 ** 14, 2, FP16)
=> ([2], 16384.0)
--- check case | Recovering after an overflow
run_scaler([0.01, 0.01, 3.0, 0.01, 0.01, 0.01, 0.01], 2.0 ** 14, 2, FP16)
=> ([2], 65536.0)
--- check case | A start that is too high halves until it fits
run_scaler([0.5, 0.5, 0.5, 0.5], 2.0 ** 18, 1000, FP16)
=> ([0, 1], 65536.0)
--- check case | A NaN gradient is skipped too
run_scaler([0.01, float("nan"), 0.01], 1024.0, 100, FP16)
=> ([1], 512.0)
--- check case | No steps
run_scaler([], 8.0, 3, FP16)
=> ([], 8.0)
--- check case | In bf16 the same gradients never overflow
run_scaler([10.0] * 3, 2.0 ** 16, 2, BF16)
=> ([], 131072.0)

+++ practice | fp32 to bf16, bit by bit
--- task
Hardware converts fp32 to bf16 with integer arithmetic on the bit pattern: bf16 is the top 16 bits of the fp32 pattern, rounded to nearest, ties to even. With `bits` the 32-bit pattern as a whole number, the rounded top half is

(bits + 0x7FFF + ((bits >> 16) & 1)) >> 16

Adding 0x7FFF (one less than half of the 16 low bits' range) carries into bit 16 when the low half is more than halfway; the extra `(bits >> 16) & 1` adds one more exactly when the kept part is odd, so a tie rounds to even. A carry out of the mantissa correctly moves into the exponent.

`struct` converts between floats and bit patterns: `struct.unpack("<I", struct.pack("<f", x))[0]` is the 32-bit pattern of x stored as fp32, and `struct.unpack("<f", struct.pack("<I", p))[0]` turns a 32-bit pattern back into a float.

Write `bf16_bits(x)`, the 16-bit bf16 pattern (a whole number from 0 to 65535) of the fp32 value of `x`. Any NaN becomes the pattern `0x7FC0` (the rounding trick could carry a NaN into infinity). Then write `bf16_value(b)`, the float a bf16 pattern stands for: put its 16 bits on top of 16 zero bits.

For example, `bf16_bits(1.0)` is `0x3F80` and `bf16_bits(-2.5)` is `0xC020`.
--- starter
import math
import struct


def bf16_bits(x):
    return 0


def bf16_value(b):
    return 0.0
--- solution
import math
import struct


def bf16_bits(x):
    if math.isnan(x):
        return 0x7FC0
    bits = struct.unpack("<I", struct.pack("<f", x))[0]
    return ((bits + 0x7FFF + ((bits >> 16) & 1)) >> 16) & 0xFFFF


def bf16_value(b):
    return struct.unpack("<f", struct.pack("<I", b << 16))[0]
--- hint
Check for NaN first. Then get the 32-bit pattern with `struct`, apply the rounding formula from the task, and keep 16 bits with `& 0xFFFF`.
--- hint
For `bf16_value`, shift the pattern left by 16 and unpack it as a 32-bit float.
--- check case | Simple values
(hex(bf16_bits(1.0)), hex(bf16_bits(-2.5)), hex(bf16_bits(0.1)))
=> ("0x3f80", "0xc020", "0x3dcd")
--- check case | Ties go to even
(hex(bf16_bits(1 + 2 ** -8)), hex(bf16_bits(1 + 3 * 2 ** -8)))
=> ("0x3f80", "0x3f82")
--- check case | Special values
(hex(bf16_bits(float("inf"))), hex(bf16_bits(float("-inf"))), hex(bf16_bits(-0.0)), hex(bf16_bits(float("nan"))))
=> ("0x7f80", "0xff80", "0x8000", "0x7fc0")
--- check case | fp32's largest number rounds up to infinity
hex(bf16_bits(3.4028234663852886e38))
=> "0x7f80"
--- check case | Decoding patterns
(bf16_value(0x3F80), bf16_value(0xC020), bf16_value(0x7F80), str(bf16_value(0x8000)))
=> (1.0, -2.5, float("inf"), "-0.0")
--- check test | Agrees with rounding the value directly, on 2,000 random fp32 numbers
(lambda st, r: (lambda vals: all(bf16_value(bf16_bits(v)) == (lambda a: 0.0 if a == 0 else __import__("math").copysign(round(abs(a) / 2.0 ** (max(__import__("math").frexp(abs(a))[1] - 1, -126) - 7)) * 2.0 ** (max(__import__("math").frexp(abs(a))[1] - 1, -126) - 7), a))(v) for v in vals))([st.unpack("<f", st.pack("<f", r.uniform(-1e4, 1e4) * 10 ** r.randint(-30, 30)))[0] for _ in range(2000)]))(__import__("struct"), __import__("random").Random(7))

+++ practice | Debug: the rounding that always goes up on a tie
--- task
`to_format(x, man_bits, min_power)` rounds a positive float `x` to a format with `man_bits` mantissa bits whose smallest normal is 2 to the power `min_power` (ignore overflow). It should round ties to even, like the hardware.

It mostly works, but `to_format(1 + 2 ** -8, 7, -126)` (exactly halfway between the bf16 numbers 1.0 and 1.0078125) returns `1.0078125` instead of `1.0`, and `to_format(2049.0, 10, -14)` returns `2050.0` instead of `2048.0`. Over millions of operations that bias pushes every sum upward. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
import math


def to_format(x, man_bits, min_power):
    power = max(math.frexp(x)[1] - 1, min_power)
    spacing = 2.0 ** (power - man_bits)
    return int(x / spacing + 0.5) * spacing
--- solution
import math


def to_format(x, man_bits, min_power):
    power = max(math.frexp(x)[1] - 1, min_power)
    spacing = 2.0 ** (power - man_bits)
    return round(x / spacing) * spacing
--- hint
What does `int(v + 0.5)` do when v is exactly 204.5? And 205.5?
--- hint
Adding one half and truncating rounds every tie up. Python's `round` rounds ties to the even neighbour.
--- check case | The ties from the task
(to_format(1 + 2 ** -8, 7, -126), to_format(2049.0, 10, -14))
=> (1.0, 2048.0)
--- check case | Ties towards an even neighbour above
(to_format(1 + 3 * 2 ** -8, 7, -126), to_format(2051.0, 10, -14))
=> (1.015625, 2052.0)
--- check case | Ordinary rounding is unchanged
(to_format(0.1, 7, -126), to_format(0.1, 10, -14))
=> (0.10009765625, 0.0999755859375)
--- check case | A subnormal tie rounds to zero
to_format(2 ** -25, 10, -14)
=> 0.0

+++ practice | fp8 with a per-tensor scale
--- task
fp8 e4m3 reaches only from about 0.002 to 448, so a tensor is scaled into that range before it is converted. Write `fp8_roundtrip(values, fmt)`:

1. amax is the largest absolute value in `values`. The scale is `largest(fmt) / amax`, or `1.0` if amax is 0 (or the list is empty).
2. Each value v becomes `round_to(v * scale, fmt) / scale`: converted to fp8 and back.
3. The error is the relative root-mean-square error: √(Σ(q − v)² / Σv²) over all values, or `0.0` if Σv² is 0.

Return `(scale, error)` with the error rounded to 4 places. The starter has `round_to`, `largest` and the formats, and a function `plain_error(values, fmt)` that computes the same error with no scaling, for comparison.

For example, `fp8_roundtrip([1e-4, 3e-4, -2e-4, 5e-5], E4M3)` has error 0.0217, while `plain_error` of the same list is 1.0: unscaled, every value rounds to zero.
--- starter
import math

E5M2 = (5, 2, True)
E4M3 = (4, 3, False)


def bias(fmt):
    return 2 ** (fmt[0] - 1) - 1


def largest(fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if has_inf:
        return (2 - 2.0 ** -man_bits) * 2.0 ** bias(fmt)
    return (2 - 2.0 ** (1 - man_bits)) * 2.0 ** (bias(fmt) + 1)


def round_to(x, fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if math.isnan(x) or x == 0:
        return x
    top = largest(fmt)
    if math.isinf(x):
        return x if has_inf else math.copysign(top, x)
    a = abs(x)
    power = max(math.frexp(a)[1] - 1, 1 - bias(fmt))
    spacing = 2.0 ** (power - man_bits)
    q = round(a / spacing) * spacing
    if q > top:
        q = math.inf if has_inf else top
    return math.copysign(q, x)


def plain_error(values, fmt):
    num = sum((round_to(v, fmt) - v) ** 2 for v in values)
    den = sum(v * v for v in values)
    return round(math.sqrt(num / den), 4) if den > 0 else 0.0


def fp8_roundtrip(values, fmt):
    return 1.0, plain_error(values, fmt)
--- solution
import math

E5M2 = (5, 2, True)
E4M3 = (4, 3, False)


def bias(fmt):
    return 2 ** (fmt[0] - 1) - 1


def largest(fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if has_inf:
        return (2 - 2.0 ** -man_bits) * 2.0 ** bias(fmt)
    return (2 - 2.0 ** (1 - man_bits)) * 2.0 ** (bias(fmt) + 1)


def round_to(x, fmt):
    exp_bits, man_bits, has_inf = fmt
    if math.isnan(x) or x == 0:
        return x
    top = largest(fmt)
    if math.isinf(x):
        return x if has_inf else math.copysign(top, x)
    a = abs(x)
    power = max(math.frexp(a)[1] - 1, 1 - bias(fmt))
    spacing = 2.0 ** (power - man_bits)
    q = round(a / spacing) * spacing
    if q > top:
        q = math.inf if has_inf else top
    return math.copysign(q, x)


def plain_error(values, fmt):
    num = sum((round_to(v, fmt) - v) ** 2 for v in values)
    den = sum(v * v for v in values)
    return round(math.sqrt(num / den), 4) if den > 0 else 0.0


def fp8_roundtrip(values, fmt):
    amax = max((abs(v) for v in values), default=0.0)
    scale = largest(fmt) / amax if amax > 0 else 1.0
    back = [round_to(v * scale, fmt) / scale for v in values]
    num = sum((q - v) ** 2 for q, v in zip(back, values))
    den = sum(v * v for v in values)
    error = math.sqrt(num / den) if den > 0 else 0.0
    return scale, round(error, 4)
--- hint
`max((abs(v) for v in values), default=0.0)` gives amax and handles the empty list.
--- hint
Compute every round-tripped value first, then the two sums. Divide by the scale after rounding, not before.
--- check case | Tiny gradients survive only with a scale
(fp8_roundtrip([1e-4, 3e-4, -2e-4, 5e-5], E4M3)[1], plain_error([1e-4, 3e-4, -2e-4, 5e-5], E4M3))
=> (0.0217, 1.0)
--- check test | The scale maps amax onto 448
abs(fp8_roundtrip([0.3, -1.7, 2.2, 0.05, -0.9], E4M3)[0] - 448 / 2.2) < 1e-9
--- check case | Ordinary activations: scaling still helps
(fp8_roundtrip([0.3, -1.7, 2.2, 0.05, -0.9], E4M3)[1], plain_error([0.3, -1.7, 2.2, 0.05, -0.9], E4M3))
=> (0.0157, 0.0259)
--- check case | Big values would saturate without the scale
(fp8_roundtrip([3000.0, -10.0, 1.0], E4M3)[1], plain_error([3000.0, -10.0, 1.0], E4M3))
=> (0.0, 0.8507)
--- check case | All zeros and an empty list
(fp8_roundtrip([0.0, 0.0], E4M3), fp8_roundtrip([], E4M3))
=> ((1.0, 0.0), (1.0, 0.0))
--- check case | e5m2 has more range but less precision
fp8_roundtrip([1e-4, 3e-4, -2e-4, 5e-5], E5M2)[1]
=> 0.0434

=== mlsys1-03 | The memory of a training step, and trading compute for it
--- teach
Lesson 01 asked how long a step takes; lesson 02 chose 2-byte numbers to make it faster. This lesson asks the question that decides most training designs: **does the step fit in the accelerator's 80 GB at all?** You will count every byte a training step needs, find that a 7-billion-parameter model does not fit on one device even before it sees any data, and build the first tool for squeezing memory: recomputing activations instead of storing them.

### What a training step keeps in memory

In AI from scratch, your training loop did four things: a forward pass, the loss, `backward()`, and an optimizer step with AdamW. Each needs memory that lives on the accelerator:

- the **weights** (parameters), read in the forward and backward passes;
- the **gradients**, one per weight, written by the backward pass;
- the **optimizer state**: AdamW keeps two running averages per weight, the first moment m and the second moment v;
- the **activations**: the intermediate results of the forward pass that the backward pass needs. Your autograd `Value` kept its children for exactly this reason.

The first three are the **model state**. Their size depends only on the number of parameters. Activations depend on how much data is in flight.

### Bytes per parameter

With lesson 02's mixed-precision recipe and AdamW, each parameter costs:

| what | format | bytes |
|---|---|---|
| weight used in forward and backward | bf16 | 2 |
| gradient | bf16 | 2 |
| master weight | fp32 | 4 |
| Adam first moment m | fp32 | 4 |
| Adam second moment v | fp32 | 4 |
| **total** | | **16** |

So **16 bytes per parameter**, and 12 of them are the optimizer's. A 7-billion-parameter model needs 7 × 10⁹ × 16 = 112 GB of model state. One 80 GB accelerator cannot even hold it. The [[optimizer state|optimizer-state]] is the biggest piece, which is why lesson 05 shards it first.

### Counting a transformer's parameters

Use the GPT-style block from AI from scratch, with hidden width h:

- attention has four h × h matrices (Wq, Wk, Wv, Wo): 4h²;
- the MLP goes from h to 4h and back: 4h² + 4h² = 8h².

That is **12h² per layer**. Biases and layer-norm scales add only a few h per layer, so leave them out. The token embedding is a V × h table for a vocabulary of V tokens; GPT-style models reuse it as the output layer (**[[tied|tied]]** embeddings), so it counts once. A model with L layers has about

**N = 12·L·h² + V·h** parameters.

Example: L = 32, h = 2560, V = 50,304 gives 12 · 32 · 2560² + 50,304 · 2560 = 2,516,582,400 + 128,778,240 ≈ 2.65 × 10⁹. Its model state is 16 × 2.65 × 10⁹ ≈ 42.3 GB.

### Counting activations

Activations are counted per layer, for a **micro-batch** of b sequences of s tokens each (b·s tokens go through the model together). With a attention heads, 16-bit activations and 1-byte dropout masks, a [[careful count|careful-count]] of everything one GPT layer saves for its backward pass comes to

**s · b · (34·h + 5·a·s) bytes per layer.**

| part of the layer | bytes per token |
|---|---|
| attention: inputs to the four multiplies, output dropout mask | 11h |
| attention scores: softmax output, its dropout mask, the dropout output | 5·a·s |
| MLP: inputs to both multiplies, the GELU input, a dropout mask | 19h |
| two layer norms: their inputs | 4h |

The 5·a·s term is the s × s attention-score matrix for each of the a heads. It grows with the **square** of the sequence length, so long sequences are dominated by it.

For the example model with a = 32, s = 2048 and b = 1:

```python
layers, width, heads, tokens = 32, 2560, 32, 2048
per_layer = tokens * (34 * width + 5 * heads * tokens)
print(per_layer / 1e9)               # 0.849 GB per layer
print(layers * per_layer / 1e9)      # 27.2 GB for the whole model
print(42.3 + 27.2)                   # 69.5 GB with the model state: it fits in 80
```

Two sequences per micro-batch would need 96.7 GB, and would not.

### Micro-batches and gradient accumulation

The optimizer may want a **global batch** of, say, 512 sequences per step, far more than fits. So the step is split: run forward and backward on a micro-batch, *add* its gradients into the gradient buffer, repeat with the next micro-batch, and only then take one optimizer step. This is **gradient accumulation**. Gradients are sums over examples, so summing micro-batch by micro-batch gives the same gradient as one huge batch, while only one micro-batch's activations are alive at a time. The global batch must be a whole number of micro-batches.

### Recomputation: trading compute for memory

Activations are kept only because backward needs them. They could instead be computed again. **Activation recomputation** (also called **[[activation checkpointing|checkpointing-name]]**) stores a few chosen tensors, called checkpoints, and throws the rest away; during the backward pass it reruns the forward pass from the nearest checkpoint to rebuild what it needs.

The usual choice for transformers is **full recomputation per layer**: keep only each layer's input, 2·s·b·h bytes, and when backward reaches a layer, rerun that layer's forward pass first. Then memory holds L layer inputs plus **one** layer's full activations at a time:

**L · 2·s·b·h + s·b·(34·h + 5·a·s)** bytes.

For the example model that is 1.18 GB instead of 27.2 GB.

What it costs: a forward pass is about 2 FLOPs per parameter per token, and backward about 4 (it computes gradients for both inputs and weights). Rerunning the forward adds 2 to the 6, about **33% more compute**.

A cheaper middle ground is **selective recomputation**: recompute only the attention scores, the 5·a·s term, which are big in memory but cheap to compute. Then each layer keeps **34·s·b·h** bytes: 5.7 GB for the example model, for a few percent extra compute.

### Where to put checkpoints

For a chain of n equal layers, checkpoint every k-th layer. Backward then holds n/k checkpoints plus the k layers of one segment being rebuilt. Memory n/k + k (in units of one layer) is smallest at k ≈ √n, which gives **O(√n) memory for one extra forward pass**, a [[classic result|sqrt-n]]. For transformers, a layer's input (2·s·b·h) is tiny next to its activations (34·s·b·h and more), so the best segment is one layer: checkpoint every layer, as the full scheme above does.

**Watch out:**

- **The model state is per device.** Data parallelism (next lessons) copies it to every device; it does not split it unless you shard it.
- **The quadratic term.** Doubling the sequence length more than doubles activation memory; at long sequences the 5·a·s term dominates.
- **Recomputation is not free.** Full recomputation costs a third more compute. Use it when memory forces you to, and prefer selective recomputation when that is enough.
- **Real memory is a bit worse** than these counts: temporary buffers, communication buffers and allocator fragmentation take several more gigabytes, so leave a margin.

::: context optimizer-state Why Adam keeps two numbers
Adam scales each weight's step by its gradient's recent history: the first moment m is a running average of the gradient, which smooths noisy steps, and the second moment v is a running average of its square, which shrinks steps for weights whose gradients are large. Both must persist from step to step, and both are kept in fp32, because v especially can be tiny and its square root divides the update. Memory-saving variants store them in 8 bits with per-block scales (lesson 02's idea), or factor v into row and column averages.
:::

::: context tied One table, two jobs
The embedding table turns each token id into a vector of width h at the bottom of the model; the output layer turns the final vector back into one score per vocabulary token at the top. Both are V × h matrices, and GPT-2 and many models since use the same matrix for both, which saves V·h parameters and tends to help small models. Some large models untie them; then the count is 12·L·h² + 2·V·h. For a 7B-class model the embedding is only about 3% of the parameters either way.
:::

::: context careful-count Where 34 and 5 come from
This count comes from a 2022 analysis of activation memory in large transformers by Korthikanti and colleagues. It assumes 16-bit activations (2 bytes each), 1-byte dropout masks, and the standard GPT layer: for example the MLP saves its 2·h-byte input, the 8·h-byte input to GELU (4h numbers of 2 bytes), the 8·h-byte input to the second multiply and an h-byte dropout mask, 19h in all. Modern attention kernels never store the s × s scores at all, recomputing them in the backward pass, which is selective recomputation built into the kernel.
:::

::: context checkpointing-name Two meanings of checkpoint
"Checkpoint" means two unrelated things in training, and both appear in this course. Activation checkpointing, here, is about memory inside one step: which activations to keep for the backward pass. Model checkpointing, in lesson 09, is about surviving failures: saving the whole model and optimizer state to storage every so often, so a crashed run can restart. Frameworks use the same word for both, so read which one a setting controls.
:::

::: context sqrt-n Square-root memory
Tianqi Chen and colleagues popularised the √n schedule for deep networks in 2016, showing that a network of n layers can be trained with O(√n) activation memory at the cost of one extra forward pass. The argument is the one in this lesson: with segments of k layers, memory is about n/k checkpoints plus k layers of one segment, and n/k + k is smallest when k = √n, where it equals 2√n. Going further, recursive schemes reach O(log n) memory by recomputing more than once, but the extra compute is rarely worth it.
:::
--- task
Build the memory model of a training step. The starter has the `Accelerator` and `ACCEL` from lesson 01 and a `Model` class holding `name`, `layers`, `hidden`, `heads` and `vocab`. Write:

- `Model.params(self)`: 12·L·h² + V·h.
- `state_bytes(model)`: the model state, 16 bytes per parameter.
- `activation_bytes(model, seq, micro_batch, recompute)`: the activation bytes of the whole model for a micro-batch of `micro_batch` sequences of `seq` tokens, with `recompute` one of
  - `"none"`: L · s·b·(34h + 5·a·s);
  - `"selective"`: L · 34·s·b·h;
  - `"full"`: L · 2·s·b·h + s·b·(34h + 5·a·s).

  Raise `ValueError` for any other `recompute`.
- `step_bytes(model, seq, micro_batch, recompute)`: model state plus activations.
- `max_micro_batch(accel, model, seq, recompute)`: the largest whole number b ≥ 0 with `step_bytes(...)` at most `accel.mem_bytes` (0 if not even one sequence fits).

Keep the counts as whole numbers: write 34h + 5·a·s inside the product rather than dividing by h. For example, `Model("2.7B", 32, 2560, 32, 50304).params()` is `2645360640`.
--- starter
class Accelerator:
    def __init__(self, name, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.name = name
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes

    def time(self, flops, nbytes):
        return max(flops / self.peak_flops, nbytes / self.mem_bw)


ACCEL = Accelerator("A-1", 1.0e15, 3.0e12, 80e9)


class Model:
    """The shape of a GPT-style transformer."""

    def __init__(self, name, layers, hidden, heads, vocab):
        self.name = name
        self.layers = layers
        self.hidden = hidden
        self.heads = heads
        self.vocab = vocab

    def params(self):
        return 0


def state_bytes(model):
    return 0


def activation_bytes(model, seq, micro_batch, recompute):
    return 0


def step_bytes(model, seq, micro_batch, recompute):
    return 0


def max_micro_batch(accel, model, seq, recompute):
    return 0
--- solution
class Accelerator:
    def __init__(self, name, peak_flops, mem_bw, mem_bytes):
        self.name = name
        self.peak_flops = peak_flops
        self.mem_bw = mem_bw
        self.mem_bytes = mem_bytes

    def time(self, flops, nbytes):
        return max(flops / self.peak_flops, nbytes / self.mem_bw)


ACCEL = Accelerator("A-1", 1.0e15, 3.0e12, 80e9)


class Model:
    """The shape of a GPT-style transformer."""

    def __init__(self, name, layers, hidden, heads, vocab):
        self.name = name
        self.layers = layers
        self.hidden = hidden
        self.heads = heads
        self.vocab = vocab

    def params(self):
        return 12 * self.layers * self.hidden ** 2 + self.vocab * self.hidden


def state_bytes(model):
    return 16 * model.params()


def layer_bytes(model, seq, micro_batch):
    return seq * micro_batch * (34 * model.hidden + 5 * model.heads * seq)


def activation_bytes(model, seq, micro_batch, recompute):
    if recompute == "none":
        return model.layers * layer_bytes(model, seq, micro_batch)
    if recompute == "selective":
        return model.layers * 34 * seq * micro_batch * model.hidden
    if recompute == "full":
        return model.layers * 2 * seq * micro_batch * model.hidden + layer_bytes(model, seq, micro_batch)
    raise ValueError(f"unknown recompute: {recompute}")


def step_bytes(model, seq, micro_batch, recompute):
    return state_bytes(model) + activation_bytes(model, seq, micro_batch, recompute)


def max_micro_batch(accel, model, seq, recompute):
    b = 0
    while step_bytes(model, seq, b + 1, recompute) <= accel.mem_bytes:
        b += 1
    return b
--- hint
Write a helper for one layer's full activations, `seq * micro_batch * (34 * hidden + 5 * heads * seq)`; the "none" and "full" cases both use it.
--- hint
`max_micro_batch` can count up from 0 while one more sequence still fits. Check `ValueError` is raised before any arithmetic for a bad `recompute`.
--- check case | Parameter counts of four model sizes
(Model("1.3B", 24, 2048, 16, 50304).params(), Model("2.7B", 32, 2560, 32, 50304).params(), Model("7B", 32, 4096, 32, 50304).params(), Model("70B", 80, 8192, 64, 50304).params())
=> (1310982144, 2645360640, 6648496128, 64836599808)
--- check case | Model state is 16 bytes a parameter: a 7B model needs 106 GB
(state_bytes(Model("1.3B", 24, 2048, 16, 50304)), state_bytes(Model("7B", 32, 4096, 32, 50304)))
=> (20975714304, 106375938048)
--- check case | Activations of the 1.3B model, three ways
[activation_bytes(Model("1.3B", 24, 2048, 16, 50304), 2048, 1, r) for r in ("none", "selective", "full")]
=> [11475615744, 3422552064, 679477248]
--- check case | Activations grow with the micro-batch
activation_bytes(Model("1.3B", 24, 2048, 16, 50304), 2048, 3, "none")
=> 34426847232
--- check case | The quadratic term: four times the sequence, over twelve times the memory
(activation_bytes(Model("1.3B", 24, 2048, 16, 50304), 8192, 1, "none"), activation_bytes(Model("1.3B", 24, 2048, 16, 50304), 8192, 1, "selective"))
=> (142539227136, 13690208256)
--- check case | Step memory for the lesson's example

=> 69504860160
--- check case | How many sequences fit, by recompute strategy

=> [5, 17, 86]
--- check case | A 7B model does not fit at all, whatever you recompute
